Yeah… I mean, I guess I do. It’s not always like I’m thinking, “Oh, I’m anxious,” but my body is already doing it. Even in the break room, where I should just be drinking coffee and minding my own business, I’m watching the doors. Listening for the machines to kick or bang all of a sudden.

Yeah… yes. It’s not like regular worrying, though. It’s more like something catches me—like a cart clanking in the hall, or that hot oil smell from the machines—and then I’m not really there anymore, not fully.

Yes… I do. I try not to, but I do.

At work I’ll take the long way around so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. It’s ridiculous, because I know the building, I know where everything is, but if I even get close to that area my stomach just—well. So I go down by shipping instead, even if it adds time.

And I don’t really talk about the accident. If people bring it up, I’ll change the subject or say I’ve got to get back to something. I used to go to these industrial-history talks, you know, old factory equipment and photos, the kind of thing most people would find boring—but I liked them. Then they showed pictures of old machinery one time, and I just couldn’t sit there. I stopped going after that.

Yes… the inspection bay, mostly. I’ll walk all the way around if I have to, just so I don’t pass it. People probably think I’m being inefficient, and I used to be so careful about saving steps at work—my mother was like that too, always saying “don’t make two trips if one will do,” which is funny what sticks in your head.

But yes, I avoid that area. And I don’t talk about the accident if I can get away with it. If someone brings it up, I’ll change the subject.

I also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I liked. They had old machinery photos one time, and it just… set me off. So I haven’t gone back.

Mostly the same things, I guess… I really do plan my route so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. It sounds silly, but I’ll take the long way around, even if it makes no sense.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… especially at work. I don’t mean to be that way, but I keep snapping at people.

There was a safety meeting where I just started yelling before I could stop myself. I could hear myself doing it, and still couldn’t seem to pull it back. My father used to raise his voice like that over the smallest thing—burnt toast, a wrench not put back right—and I always hated it, so it’s not exactly nice realizing I’m doing something similar.

Now some of the younger inspectors look nervous to ask me things, like they’re afraid I’ll bite their head off. That bothers me. I’m not trying to scare anybody. It just comes out so fast.

I’ll walk all the way around if I have to, just so I don’t pass it. People probably think I’m being inefficient, and I used to be so careful about saving steps at work—my mother was like that too, always saying “don’t make two trips if one will do,” which is funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut yes, I avoid that area. And I don’t talk about the accident if I can get away with it. If someone brings it up, I’ll change the subject.\n\nI also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I liked. They had old machinery photos one time, and it just… set me off. So I haven’t gone back.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or places you've stopped doing or going to because they remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Mostly the same things, I guess… I really do plan my route so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. It sounds silly, but I’ll take the long way around, even if it makes no sense.\n\nAnd I don’t like talking about the accident. If someone starts in about it, I’ll find some excuse, or ask about their kids, or the weather—my aunt used to do that, change the subject so smooth you didn’t notice till later. I suppose I picked that up.\n\nI also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to enjoy. They had pictures of old machines one time, and that was enough for me. I just never went back.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… especially at work. I don’t mean to be that way, but I keep snapping at people.\n\nThere was a safety meeting where I just started yelling before I could stop myself. I could hear myself doing it, and still couldn’t seem to pull it back. My father used to raise his voice like that over the smallest thing—burnt toast, a wrench not put back right—and I always hated it, so it’s not exactly nice realizing I’m doing something similar.\n\nNow some of the younger inspectors look nervous to ask me things, like they’re afraid I’ll bite their head off. That bothers me. I’m not trying to scare anybody. It just comes out so fast.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had difficulty performing your job as an inspector since the accident?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’ve missed so many shifts and lost so much patience with trainees that I’m no longer trusted to mentor the younger inspectors, and I’ve pulled back from community theater because I can’t reliably get through rehearsals.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I have. More than I like to admit.

I’ve missed a lot of shifts since it happened—too many, really. And when I am there, I don’t have the patience I used to, especially with the trainees. I used to be the one they’d put with the younger inspectors, you know, to show them how to check things properly. I was proud of that, actually. My old supervisor used to say I had “eyes like a jeweler,” which always made me laugh because I can’t even thread a needle anymore without my glasses.

But now they don’t really trust me to mentor them. Not like before. I lose my temper, or I get short, and people notice.

And outside of work too… I pulled back from community theater. I used to help with rehearsals, little parts, props sometimes—nothing fancy. But I can’t reliably get through rehearsals anymore, so I stopped going as much. It’s embarrassing.

People are just getting sheet music or moving chairs, and I know that, but I’m still tracking it. It’s like my body won’t believe I’m safe, even when nothing is happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same things or feeling like you need to repeat certain actions over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I do anything like counting or washing my hands over and over, if that’s what you mean. I do get stuck on things sometimes, but—well, everybody does, right? I guess I’m not really sure what you mean by repeating actions. Nothing like a routine I have to do a certain way, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not like regular worrying, though. It’s more like something catches me—like a cart clanking in the hall, or that hot oil smell from the machines—and then I’m not really there anymore, not fully.\n\nI’m right back in that bay. I can see my coworker by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me again. And I try to shake it off, you know, tell myself I’m at work now, or I’m just walking past maintenance, but it sticks in my head. Then they showed pictures of old machinery one time, and I just couldn’t sit there. I stopped going after that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. A cart will clank in the hall, or I’ll catch that smell, that hot oil smell from the machines, and all of a sudden I’m not just remembering it.\n\nI’m back in that bay. I can see my coworker by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again. It’s strange, because I know where I am, I know I’m standing there now, but for a few moments it’s like my head doesn’t believe it.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice something stupid, like the floor tiles or the clipboard in my hand, and I try to use that to pull myself back. Pretty much since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you avoid because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… the inspection bay, mostly. I’ll walk all the way around if I have to, just so I don’t pass it. And I don’t talk about the accident if I can get away with it. If someone brings it up, I’ll change the subject.\n\nI also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I liked. They had old machinery photos one time, and it just… set me off. I used to be the one they’d put with the younger inspectors, you know, to show them how to check things properly. I was proud of that, actually. My old supervisor used to say I had “eyes like a jeweler,” which always made me laugh because I can’t even thread a needle anymore without my glasses.\n\nBut now they don’t really trust me to mentor them. Not like before. I lose my temper, or I get short, and people notice.\n\nAnd outside of work too… I pulled back from community theater. I used to help with rehearsals, little parts, props sometimes—nothing fancy. But I can’t reliably get through rehearsals anymore, so I stopped going as much. It’s embarrassing.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any other activities or hobbies you've given up because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… mostly things with machinery in them. I used to like those industrial-history talks—old factories, old equipment, that sort of thing. My uncle used to drag us to museums like that when we were kids, and I guess I kept the habit.

But after the accident, they showed pictures of old machines at one of those talks, and I just… couldn’t sit there. It set me off too much. So I quit going.

And I know I already mentioned it, but I really do go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay. I’ll take some ridiculous route around it. And if people start talking about the accident, I shut it down or change the subject. I just don’t want it in my head if I can help it.

It just came out.\n\nNow the younger inspectors, they look at me different. Like they’re scared to ask me anything. I don’t want to be that person, but I keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I do. It’s not always like I’m thinking, “Oh, I’m anxious,” but my body is already doing it. Even in the break room, where I should just be drinking coffee and minding my own business, I’m watching the doors. Listening for the machines to kick or bang all of a sudden.\n\nAnd at rehearsal too—sorry, I sing with a little group, it’s nothing fancy—but even there I’m checking who’s moving behind me. People are just getting sheet music or moving chairs, and I know that, but I’m still tracking it. It’s like my body won’t believe I’m safe, even when nothing is happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same things or feeling like you need to repeat certain actions over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I do anything like counting or washing my hands over and over, if that’s what you mean. I do get stuck on things sometimes, but—well, everybody does, right? Like if there’s a mistake on a report, I’ll keep thinking about who signed off on it, and then I remember this one time the copier jammed right before lunch and somehow that turned into a whole argument in the office…\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess I’m not really sure what you mean by repeating actions. Nothing like a routine I have to do a certain way, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not like regular worrying, though. It’s more like something catches me—like a cart clanking in the hall, or that hot oil smell from the machines—and then I’m not really there anymore, not fully.\n\nI’m right back in that bay. I can see my coworker by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me again. And I try to shake it off, you know, tell myself I’m at work now, or I’m just walking past maintenance, but it sticks in my head. Then the whole rest of the day feels… off.\n\nSorry, I know you just asked about thoughts. But yeah. They get in there and I can’t get them out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of something unpleasant?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I try not to, but I do.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. It’s ridiculous, because I know the building, I know where everything is, but if I even get close to that area my stomach just—well. So I go down by shipping instead, even if it adds time.\n\nAnd I don’t really talk about the accident. If people bring it up, I’ll change the subject or say I’ve got to get back to something. I used to go to these industrial-history talks, you know, old factory equipment and photos, the kind of thing most people would find boring—but I liked them. Then they showed pictures of old machinery one time, and I just couldn’t sit there. I stopped going after that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. A cart will clank in the hall, or I’ll catch that smell, that hot oil smell from the machines, and all of a sudden I’m not just remembering it.\n\nI’m back in that bay. I can see my coworker by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again. It’s strange, because I know where I am, I know I’m standing there now, but for a few moments it’s like my head doesn’t believe it.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice something stupid, like the floor tiles or the clipboard in my hand, and I try to use that to pull myself back. Pretty much since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you avoid because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… the inspection bay, mostly. I’ll walk all the way around if I have to, just so I don’t pass it. And I don’t talk about the accident if I can get away with it. If someone brings it up, I’ll change the subject.\n\nI also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I liked. They had old machinery photos one time, and it just… set me off. So I haven’t gone back.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or places you've stopped doing or going to because they remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Mostly the same things, I guess… I really do plan my route so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. industrial-history talks I used to enjoy because the old machinery pictures set me off.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I remember the shift and I remember people yelling afterward, but there’s a whole stretch between the first alarm and seeing him down that is just blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I don’t trust that plant or the people running it anymore, and some days I feel like I’m damaged goods who can’t even do the job I spent my life getting good at.\n- key: S073\n  name: Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame\n  description: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.\n  manifestation: I keep thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks or stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I’m here because I keep snapping at people, like at that safety meeting when I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to ask me anything.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: Even in the break room or at rehearsal, I’m watching doors, listening for sudden machine noises, and checking who’s moving behind me because my body won’t believe I’m safe.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve missed so many shifts and lost so much patience with trainees that I’m no longer trusted to mentor the younger inspectors, and I’ve pulled back from community theater because I can’t reliably get through rehearsals.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since that machinery accident after the overnight schedule change, about 2 years and 4 months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S077\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… especially at work. I don’t mean to be that way, but I keep snapping at people.\n\nThere was a safety meeting where I just started yelling before I could stop myself. I could hear myself doing it, and still couldn’t seem to pull it back. My father used to raise his voice like that over the smallest thing—burnt toast, a wrench not put back right—and I always hated it, so it’s not exactly nice realizing I’m doing something similar.\n\nNow some of the younger inspectors look nervous to ask me things, like they’re afraid I’ll bite their head off. That bothers me. I’m not trying to scare anybody. It just comes out so fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty performing your job as an inspector since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I like to admit.\n\nI’ve missed a lot of shifts since it happened—too many, really. And when I am there, I don’t have the patience I used to, especially with the trainees. I used to be the one they’d put with the younger inspectors, you know, to show them how to check things properly. I was proud of that, actually. My old supervisor used to say I had “eyes like a jeweler,” which always made me laugh because I can’t even thread a needle anymore without my glasses.\n\nBut now they don’t really trust me to mentor them. Not like before. I lose my temper, or I get short, and people notice.\n\nAnd outside of work too… I pulled back from community theater. I used to help with rehearsals, little parts, props sometimes—nothing fancy. But I can’t reliably get through rehearsals anymore, so I stopped going as much. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or hobbies you've given up because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly things with machinery in them. I used to like those industrial-history talks—old factories, old equipment, that sort of thing. My uncle used to drag us to museums like that when we were kids, and I guess I kept the habit.\n\nBut after the accident, they showed pictures of old machines at one of those talks, and I just… couldn’t sit there. It set me off too much. So I quit going.\n\nAnd I know I already mentioned it, but I really do go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay. I’ll take some ridiculous route around it. And if people start talking about the accident, I shut it down or change the subject. I just don’t want it in my head if I can help it.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any other situations or activities you've avoided because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, it’s mostly the same cluster of things, I guess. I avoid the inspection bay if I can. I’ll take the long way around, even if it looks silly, just so I don’t have to pass it.

And I don’t like talking about the accident. If somebody brings it up, I try to change the subject or shut it down. I know that probably makes people uncomfortable, but I just… I don’t want to go back there in my head.

I also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like. They had pictures of old machinery once, and it set me off badly enough that I haven’t wanted to go back. Funny, because I used to enjoy all that old factory stuff—my uncle would’ve had a whole opinion about it, he could talk for an hour about a steam press—but now even pictures of machines are too much.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I keep snapping at people. At work, you know, I’m an inspector, and there was this safety meeting—those meetings always drag on, and half the time people don’t even listen—but I started yelling before I could stop myself. It just came out.\n\nNow the younger inspectors, they look at me different. Like they’re scared to ask me anything. I don’t want to be that person, but I keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I do. It’s not always like I’m thinking, “Oh, I’m anxious,” but my body is already doing it. Even in the break room, where I should just be drinking coffee and minding my own business, I’m watching the doors. Listening for the machines to kick or bang all of a sudden.\n\nAnd at rehearsal too—sorry, I sing with a little group, it’s nothing fancy—but even there I’m checking who’s moving behind me. People are just getting sheet music or moving chairs, and I know that, but I’m still tracking it. It’s like my body won’t believe I’m safe, even when nothing is happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same things or feeling like you need to repeat certain actions over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I do anything like counting or washing my hands over and over, if that’s what you mean. I do get stuck on things sometimes, but—well, everybody does, right? Like if there’s a mistake on a report, I’ll keep thinking about who signed off on it, and then I remember this one time the copier jammed right before lunch and somehow that turned into a whole argument in the office…\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess I’m not really sure what you mean by repeating actions. Nothing like a routine I have to do a certain way, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not like regular worrying, though. It’s more like something catches me—like a cart clanking in the hall, or that hot oil smell from the machines—and then I’m not really there anymore, not fully.\n\nI’m right back in that bay. I can see my coworker by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me again. And I try to shake it off, you know, tell myself I’m at work now, or I’m just walking past maintenance, but it sticks in my head. Then the whole rest of the day feels… off.\n\nSorry, I know you just asked about thoughts. But yeah. They get in there and I can’t get them out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of something unpleasant?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I try not to, but I do.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. It’s ridiculous, because I know the building, I know where everything is, but if I even get close to that area my stomach just—well. So I go down by shipping instead, even if it adds time.\n\nAnd I don’t really talk about the accident. If people bring it up, I’ll change the subject or say I’ve got to get back to something. I used to go to these industrial-history talks, you know, old factory equipment and photos, the kind of thing most people would find boring—but I liked them. Then they showed pictures of old machinery one time, and I just couldn’t sit there. I stopped going after that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. A cart will clank in the hall, or I’ll catch that smell, that hot oil smell from the machines, and all of a sudden I’m not just remembering it.\n\nI’m back in that bay. I can see my coworker by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again. It’s strange, because I know where I am, I know I’m standing there now, but for a few moments it’s like my head doesn’t believe it.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice something stupid, like the floor tiles or the clipboard in my hand, and I try to use that to pull myself back. But yes… it does feel like it’s happening again.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these flashbacks and feelings of being on edge?\n\nPatient: Since the machinery accident… the one after they changed us over to that overnight schedule. I keep thinking if the schedule hadn’t changed, if everybody hadn’t been tired—well, sorry.\n\nIt’s been about… two years and four months now. Pretty much since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you avoid because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… the inspection bay, mostly. I’ll walk all the way around if I have to, just so I don’t pass it. People probably think I’m being inefficient, and I used to be so careful about saving steps at work—my mother was like that too, always saying “don’t make two trips if one will do,” which is funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut yes, I avoid that area. And I don’t talk about the accident if I can get away with it. If someone brings it up, I’ll change the subject.\n\nI also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I liked. They had old machinery photos one time, and it just… set me off. So I haven’t gone back.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or places you've stopped doing or going to because they remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Mostly the same things, I guess… I really do plan my route so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. It sounds silly, but I’ll take the long way around, even if it makes no sense.\n\nAnd I don’t like talking about the accident. If someone starts in about it, I’ll find some excuse, or ask about their kids, or the weather—my aunt used to do that, change the subject so smooth you didn’t notice till later. I suppose I picked that up.\n\nI also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to enjoy. They had pictures of old machines one time, and that was enough for me. I just never went back.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… especially at work. I don’t mean to be that way, but I keep snapping at people.\n\nThere was a safety meeting where I just started yelling before I could stop myself. I could hear myself doing it, and still couldn’t seem to pull it back. My father used to raise his voice like that over the smallest thing—burnt toast, a wrench not put back right—and I always hated it, so it’s not exactly nice realizing I’m doing something similar.\n\nNow some of the younger inspectors look nervous to ask me things, like they’re afraid I’ll bite their head off. That bothers me. I’m not trying to scare anybody. It just comes out so fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty performing your job as an inspector since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I like to admit.\n\nI’ve missed a lot of shifts since it happened—too many, really. And when I am there, I don’t have the patience I used to, especially with the trainees. I used to be the one they’d put with the younger inspectors, you know, to show them how to check things properly. I was proud of that, actually. My old supervisor used to say I had “eyes like a jeweler,” which always made me laugh because I can’t even thread a needle anymore without my glasses.\n\nBut now they don’t really trust me to mentor them. Not like before. I lose my temper, or I get short, and people notice.\n\nAnd outside of work too… I pulled back from community theater. I used to help with rehearsals, little parts, props sometimes—nothing fancy. But I can’t reliably get through rehearsals anymore, so I stopped going as much. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or hobbies you've given up because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly things with machinery in them. I used to like those industrial-history talks—old factories, old equipment, that sort of thing. My uncle used to drag us to museums like that when we were kids, and I guess I kept the habit.\n\nBut after the accident, they showed pictures of old machines at one of those talks, and I just… couldn’t sit there. It set me off too much. So I quit going.\n\nAnd I know I already mentioned it, but I really do go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay. I’ll take some ridiculous route around it. And if people start talking about the accident, I shut it down or change the subject. I just don’t want it in my head if I can help it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities you've avoided because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s mostly the same cluster of things, I guess. I avoid the inspection bay if I can. I’ll take the long way around, even if it looks silly, just so I don’t have to pass it.\n\nAnd I don’t like talking about the accident. If somebody brings it up, I try to change the subject or shut it down. I know that probably makes people uncomfortable, but I just… I don’t want to go back there in my head.\n\nI also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It sounds silly, but I’ll take the long way around, even if it makes no sense.\n\nAnd I don’t like talking about the accident. If someone starts in about it, I’ll find some excuse, or ask about their kids, or the weather—my aunt used to do that, change the subject so smooth you didn’t notice till later. I suppose I picked that up.\n\nI also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to enjoy. They had pictures of old machines one time, and that was enough for me. I just never went back.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… especially at work. I don’t mean to be that way, but I keep snapping at people.\n\nThere was a safety meeting where I just started yelling before I could stop myself. I could hear myself doing it, and still couldn’t seem to pull it back. My father used to raise his voice like that over the smallest thing—burnt toast, a wrench not put back right—and I always hated it, so it’s not exactly nice realizing I’m doing something similar.\n\nNow some of the younger inspectors look nervous to ask me things, like they’re afraid I’ll bite their head off. That bothers me. I’m not trying to scare anybody. It just comes out so fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty performing your job as an inspector since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I like to admit.\n\nI’ve missed a lot of shifts since it happened—too many, really. And when I am there, I don’t have the patience I used to, especially with the trainees. I used to be the one they’d put with the younger inspectors, you know, to show them how to check things properly. I was proud of that, actually. My old supervisor used to say I had “eyes like a jeweler,” which always made me laugh because I can’t even thread a needle anymore without my glasses.\n\nBut now they don’t really trust me to mentor them. Not like before. I lose my temper, or I get short, and people notice.\n\nAnd outside of work too… I pulled back from community theater. I used to help with rehearsals, little parts, props sometimes—nothing fancy. But I can’t reliably get through rehearsals anymore, so I stopped going as much. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or hobbies you've given up because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly things with machinery in them. I used to like those industrial-history talks—old factories, old equipment, that sort of thing. My uncle used to drag us to museums like that when we were kids, and I guess I kept the habit.\n\nBut after the accident, they showed pictures of old machines at one of those talks, and I just… couldn’t sit there. It set me off too much. So I quit going.\n\nAnd I know I already mentioned it, but I really do go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay. I’ll take some ridiculous route around it. Mostly the inspection bay. I’ll go all the way around the building if I have to, which is ridiculous because I know every shortcut in that place—I used to tell the new folks, “Don’t waste steps if you don’t have to.” My dad was the same way at the grocery store, had a whole system for aisles… anyway.

Yes… I do. More than I probably should.

I keep going over it, thinking if I’d pushed harder about the safety checks, or just said, “No, stop the run, something doesn’t feel right,” then maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I mean, I know people always say you can’t know that, and at work there’s always a chain of people involved—forms, supervisors, all that paperwork, half the time the printer jams right when you need it—but in my head it still comes back to me.

I just keep thinking I should’ve done more.

And I try to shake it off, you know, tell myself I’m at work now, or I’m just walking past maintenance, but it sticks in my head. Then the whole rest of the day feels… off.\n\nSorry, I know you just asked about thoughts. But yeah. They get in there and I can’t get them out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of something unpleasant?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I try not to, but I do.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. It’s ridiculous, because I know the building, I know where everything is, but if I even get close to that area my stomach just—well. So I go down by shipping instead, even if it adds time.\n\nAnd I don’t really talk about the accident. If people bring it up, I’ll change the subject or say I’ve got to get back to something. I used to go to these industrial-history talks, you know, old factory equipment and photos, the kind of thing most people would find boring—but I liked them. Then they showed pictures of old machinery one time, and I just couldn’t sit there. I stopped going after that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. A cart will clank in the hall, or I’ll catch that smell, that hot oil smell from the machines, and all of a sudden I’m not just remembering it.\n\nI’m back in that bay. I can see my coworker by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again. It’s strange, because I know where I am, I know I’m standing there now, but for a few moments it’s like my head doesn’t believe it.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice something stupid, like the floor tiles or the clipboard in my hand, and I try to use that to pull myself back. But yes… it does feel like it’s happening again.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these flashbacks and feelings of being on edge?\n\nPatient: Since the machinery accident… the one after they changed us over to that overnight schedule. I keep thinking if the schedule hadn’t changed, if everybody hadn’t been tired—well, sorry.\n\nIt’s been about… two years and four months now. Pretty much since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you avoid because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… the inspection bay, mostly. I’ll walk all the way around if I have to, just so I don’t pass it. People probably think I’m being inefficient, and I used to be so careful about saving steps at work—my mother was like that too, always saying “don’t make two trips if one will do,” which is funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut yes, I avoid that area. And I don’t talk about the accident if I can get away with it. If someone brings it up, I’ll change the subject.\n\nI also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I liked. They had old machinery photos one time, and it just… set me off. So I haven’t gone back.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or places you've stopped doing or going to because they remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Mostly the same things, I guess… I really do plan my route so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. It sounds silly, but I’ll take the long way around, even if it makes no sense.\n\nAnd I don’t like talking about the accident. If someone starts in about it, I’ll find some excuse, or ask about their kids, or the weather—my aunt used to do that, change the subject so smooth you didn’t notice till later. I suppose I picked that up.\n\nI also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to enjoy. They had pictures of old machines one time, and that was enough for me. I just never went back.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… especially at work. I don’t mean to be that way, but I keep snapping at people.\n\nThere was a safety meeting where I just started yelling before I could stop myself. I could hear myself doing it, and still couldn’t seem to pull it back. My father used to raise his voice like that over the smallest thing—burnt toast, a wrench not put back right—and I always hated it, so it’s not exactly nice realizing I’m doing something similar.\n\nNow some of the younger inspectors look nervous to ask me things, like they’re afraid I’ll bite their head off. That bothers me. I’m not trying to scare anybody. It just comes out so fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty performing your job as an inspector since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I like to admit.\n\nI’ve missed a lot of shifts since it happened—too many, really. And when I am there, I don’t have the patience I used to, especially with the trainees. I used to be the one they’d put with the younger inspectors, you know, to show them how to check things properly. I was proud of that, actually. My old supervisor used to say I had “eyes like a jeweler,” which always made me laugh because I can’t even thread a needle anymore without my glasses.\n\nBut now they don’t really trust me to mentor them. Not like before. I lose my temper, or I get short, and people notice.\n\nAnd outside of work too… I pulled back from community theater. I used to help with rehearsals, little parts, props sometimes—nothing fancy. But I can’t reliably get through rehearsals anymore, so I stopped going as much. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or hobbies you've given up because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly things with machinery in them. I used to like those industrial-history talks—old factories, old equipment, that sort of thing. My uncle used to drag us to museums like that when we were kids, and I guess I kept the habit.\n\nBut after the accident, they showed pictures of old machines at one of those talks, and I just… couldn’t sit there. It set me off too much. So I quit going.\n\nAnd I know I already mentioned it, but I really do go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay. I’ll take some ridiculous route around it. And if people start talking about the accident, I shut it down or change the subject. I just don’t want it in my head if I can help it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities you've avoided because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s mostly the same cluster of things, I guess. I avoid the inspection bay if I can. I’ll take the long way around, even if it looks silly, just so I don’t have to pass it.\n\nAnd I don’t like talking about the accident. If somebody brings it up, I try to change the subject or shut it down. I know that probably makes people uncomfortable, but I just… I don’t want to go back there in my head.\n\nI also stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like. They had pictures of old machinery once, and it set me off badly enough that I haven’t wanted to go back. Funny, because I used to enjoy all that old factory stuff—my uncle would’ve had a whole opinion about it, he could talk for an hour about a steam press—but now even pictures of machines are too much.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places or situations that you avoid because they remind you of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Mostly the inspection bay. I’ll go all the way around the building if I have to, which is ridiculous because I know every shortcut in that place—I used to tell the new folks, “Don’t waste steps if you don’t have to.” My dad was the same way at the grocery store, had a whole system for aisles… anyway.\n\nI just don’t want to pass that bay. And I don’t want to talk about what happened. If someone brings it up, I change the subject if I can.\n\nAnd I stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like. They had pictures of old machinery once, and it just hit me wrong. I used to enjoy that stuff, old factory equipment and all that, but now I can’t sit through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed about what happened during the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably should.\n\nI keep going over it, thinking if I’d pushed harder about the safety checks, or just said, “No, stop the run, something doesn’t feel right,” then maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I mean, I know people always say you can’t know that, and at work there’s always a chain of people involved—forms, supervisors, all that paperwork, half the time the printer jams right when you need it—but in my head it still comes back to me.\n\nI just keep thinking I should’ve done more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
